## Semantic Chunks
### Section: Introduction
- **Level**: `intro`
- **Hash**: `dc3fdf1790463207179b206a8e2c069be9774d8c7c92f847283148cf8befa366`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/how-do-i-talk-to-a-student-i-suspect-of-using-ai` (Similarity: 0.9386)

```text
Yes. Asking a student to explain their writing can be a useful part of investigating possible AI use, especially when the conversation is handled as a request for clarification rather than an accusation.

A student who wrote an assignment should generally be able to discuss its main argument, sources, terminology, and writing process. That does not mean they need to remember every sentence or defend the paper like an oral examination.

The purpose is to determine whether the student's understanding is reasonably consistent with the work they submitted.

If a teacher is concerned about possible ChatGPT or other AI use, a student conversation can be considered alongside other evidence such as previous writing, document history, source verification, and AI detection results.

Checkmark Plagiarism helps teachers gather that broader evidence through AI detection , essay writing playback , static AI detection, plagiarism detection , autograding , and integrations with Canvas and Google Classroom .
```

### Section: Why Ask the Student About Their Writing?
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `d990b9268a040562266309d4bd6d58ee87929d469f9320ee4daf123a3be0a731`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/how-do-i-talk-to-a-student-i-suspect-of-using-ai` (Similarity: 0.8642)

```text
The finished text cannot always distinguish between human writing, tutor revisions, Grammarly sentence rewrites, or ChatGPT generation. Talking with the student provides qualitative insight into their familiarity with the ideas, research, and drafting process behind the submission.
```

### Section: Asking for an Explanation Is Not the Same as Accusing the Student
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `82175936ba1869d1d1e3a6b232618475d7da0018f1a1c1a882089a56e97d066c`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/how-do-i-talk-to-a-student-i-suspect-of-using-ai` (Similarity: 0.8953)

```text
Avoid opening with: "I know you used ChatGPT." A more constructive starting point is: "I had a few questions about your assignment and wanted to understand how you approached it." This frames the dialogue as evidence gathering rather than an interrogation.
```

### Section: What Should I Ask the Student?
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `48d67736d3977f204f07c4acbe669051f1200094e40fddfa8cafe08f3234fcbd`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-if-a-student-has-no-drafts-for-an-assignment` (Similarity: 0.8551)

```text
Begin with broad thematic questions before drilling down into specific passages:

What is your central thesis? 
 How did you decide on this topic? 
 Which section was most difficult to write? 
 What sources were most influential to your argument? 
 What tools did you use while drafting and revising?
```

### Section: Ask the Student to Explain the Thesis
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `e47539d6a1cddf388e02a9410f76dce33a0277abee9fade12f47a8bdca0e0f70`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-should-i-do-if-a-student-cant-explain-their-own-essay` (Similarity: 0.9200)

```text
Ask: "What are you arguing in this essay?" The student does not need to recite the exact thesis statement. Explaining the core idea in plain, conversational language is a strong sign of genuine conceptual ownership.
```

### Section: Ask About Specific Paragraphs
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `460a737b54ff8f450468697d9ab8dd93c72f74380b553347f2f3f3b5ed1b6d4e`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-questions-should-i-ask-a-student-about-a-suspicious-assignment` (Similarity: 0.8858)

```text
Select a major argumentative paragraph and ask: "Can you explain what you're saying in this paragraph and how it connects to your thesis?" Look for understanding of the reasoning rather than word-for-word memorization.
```

### Section: Ask About Advanced Vocabulary
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `72c221c48827371e77bfd78fe2fee27bc63f91a3cd36ae0c6af256c07abb841a`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-questions-should-i-ask-a-student-about-a-suspicious-assignment` (Similarity: 0.9009)

```text
If an essay contains specialized terminology like "socioeconomic stratification," "cognitive dissonance," or "epistemological framework," ask: "What does this term mean in your argument?" The student should be able to explain the concept in context.
```

### Section: Ask About Sources
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `edd5c796638dca1fea6e685b789ddf61bbf81a74f8494f7bdd02c15f26ec21dd`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-questions-should-i-ask-a-student-about-a-suspicious-assignment` (Similarity: 0.8733)

```text
Ask: "Why did you choose this source? Where did you find it? Can you show me the passage you quoted?" Inability to explain citations justifies verifying whether the cited papers and authors actually exist.
```

### Section: Ask About the Writing Process
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `a043e202cde5bbb8ced33802dbe306d84106bfc98a07b61c859d02975a972adf`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-should-i-do-if-a-student-cant-explain-their-own-essay` (Similarity: 0.9578)

```text
Ask: "Can you walk me through how you wrote the assignment from beginning to end?" Inquire about outlining, drafting location, revision cycles, and digital tools utilized.
```

### Section: Why Writing Process Matters
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `6152c5c51b25e7a7663c25d25d21960ee7ea96c441d889809bef665c62a1cadc`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/how-can-i-verify-who-actually-wrote-an-assignment` (Similarity: 0.8608)

```text
Student A (Multi-Session Growth)

Outline and rough notes in playback 
 Paragraphs drafted gradually over several days 
 Sentence rewriting & structural revisions 
 Fluently explains the thesis and sources

Student B (Unverified Shift)

Empty document receives 1,000 words instantly 
 Zero subsequent editing or revision 
 Several citations do not exist in databases 
 Cannot explain key terminology used
```

### Section: What Should I Avoid Asking?

### Section: A Practical Conversation Process
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `d5a23ac5cb3040e44bf14c52bd3531ae4dd9e073eaf6d84cefb7079f67763f92`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/how-do-i-talk-to-a-student-i-suspect-of-using-ai` (Similarity: 0.8581)

```text
A Practical 14-Step Conversation Framework:

1. Review the assignment carefully prior to scheduling the conference. 
 2. Identify specific passages, vocabulary, or citations that raise concern. 
 3. Review previous student work baselines for style and syntax comparisons. 
 4. Check AI detection results as one signal among several. 
 5. Review essay writing playback to examine drafting progression over time. 
 6. Begin the conversation neutrally: "Help me understand how you approached this." 
 7. Ask the student to summarize their central thesis in conversational terms. 
 8. Inquire about specific paragraphs, evidence, and research citations. 
 9. Ask the student to describe their step-by-step drafting and revision process. 
 10. Inquire about editing tools, writing assistants, or tutoring support utilized. 
 11. Ask neutrally about AI tools if relevant to the assignment policy. 
 12. Provide the student an opportunity to share earlier drafts or version notes. 
 13. Synthesize the student's explanation with writing history and baseline evidence. 
 14. Follow established school academic-integrity procedures if concerns remain.
```
